=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a binary string s of length n and an integer numOps.
You are allowed to perform the following operation on s at most numOps times:

Select any index i (where 0 <= i < n) and flip s[i]. If s[i] == '1', change s[i] to '0' and vice versa.

You need to minimize the length of the longest substring of s such that all the characters in the substring are identical.
Return the minimum length after the operations.
 
Example 1:

Input: s = "000001", numOps = 1
Output: 2
Explanation: 
By changing s[2] to '1', s becomes "001001". The longest substrings with identical characters are s[0..1] and s[3..4].

Example 2:

Input: s = "0000", numOps = 2
Output: 1
Explanation: 
By changing s[0] and s[2] to '1', s becomes "1010".

Example 3:

Input: s = "0101", numOps = 0
Output: 1

 
Constraints:

1 <= n == s.length <= 1000
s consists only of '0' and '1'.
0 <= numOps <= n

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Function can(L):
    if L == 0: return False? But low=1 so no.
    INF = 10**9
    # dp[c][r] after processed positions, c 0/1, r 1..L
    dp = [[INF]*(L+1) for _ in range(2)]
    # first char
    for c in (0,1):
        dp[c][1] = (s[0] != str(c))  # but s char compare int
    for ch in s[1:]:
        ndp = [[INF]*(L+1) for _ in range(2)]
        for c in (0,1):
            for r in range(1,L+1):
                cur = dp[c][r]
                if cur >= INF: continue
                # choose same c
                if r < L:
                    cost = cur + (1 if (ord(ch)-48) != c else 0)
                    if cost < ndp[c][r+1]: ndp[c][r+1] = cost
                # choose opposite
                nc = 1-c
                cost = cur + (1 if (ord(ch)-48) != nc else 0)
                if cost < ndp[nc][1]: ndp[nc][1] = cost
        dp = ndp
    return min(dp[c][r]) <= numOps

Let's scrutinize DP correctness.

Define after processing i characters (prefix length i), dp[c][r] = minimum flips among prefixes t of length i such that t has no run > L, ends with char c, and the last run of t has length exactly r (r between1 and L). For i=0, no state. For i=1, dp[c][1] = cost to set first char to c. This prefix trivially valid. Transition append char d:
- If d == c, new run length r+1. Allowed only if r < L. Cost add (s[i] != d). If r == L, cannot append same because would create run L+1.
- If d != c, new run length 1. Allowed for any r. Cost add (s[i] != d).
This enumerates all valid prefixes. At end min over c,r. So can(L) returns min flips. Correct.

Potential optimization: Instead of comparing char to int each time, convert s to list of ints once. Use s_ints = [1 if ch=='1' else 0 for ch in s]. Then cost = (s_ints[i] != c). Good.

Option:
def can(L):
    dp0 = [INF]*(L+1)
    dp1 = [INF]*(L+1)
    first = s[0]
    dp0[1] = 0 if first == 0 else 1
    dp1[1] = 0 if first == 1 else 1
    for bit in s[1:]:
        ndp0 = [INF]*(L+1)
        ndp1 = [INF]*(L+1)
        # from previous 0 states
        # choose same 0
        # for r in 1..L-1: ndp0[r+1] = min(ndp0[r+1], dp0[r] + (bit != 0))
        # choose diff 1
        # ndp1[1] = min(ndp1[1], dp0[r] + (bit != 1)) for all r
        # from previous 1 states
        # choose same 1
        # for r in 1..L-1: ndp1[r+1] = min(ndp1[r+1], dp1[r] + (bit != 1))
        # choose diff 0
        # ndp0[1] = min(ndp0[1], dp1[r] + (bit != 0)) for all r
        cost0 = 0 if bit == 0 else 1
        cost1 = 0 if bit == 1 else 1
        # same transitions
        for r in range(1, L):
            v = dp0[r] + cost0
            if v < ndp0[r+1]: ndp0[r+1] = v
            v = dp1[r] + cost1
            if v < ndp1[r+1]: ndp1[r+1] = v
        # diff transitions: min over dp0 and dp1
        min_dp0 = min(dp0[1:]) maybe min(dp0)
        min_dp1 = min(dp1[1:])
        # choose 0 after previous 1: ndp0[1] = min_dp1 + cost0
        ndp0[1] = min(ndp0[1], min_dp1 + cost0)
        ndp1[1] = min(ndp1[1], min_dp0 + cost1)
        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
    return min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps

Let's write can(L) using two arrays and min over slices maybe.

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        arr = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in s]

        def can(L: int) -> bool:
            INF = 10**9
            dp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
            dp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)
            first = arr[0]
            dp0[1] = 0 if first == 0 else 1
            dp1[1] = 0 if first == 1 else 1

            for bit in arr[1:]:
                ndp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
                ndp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)
                cost0 = 0 if bit == 0 else 1
                cost1 = 0 if bit == 1 else 1

                # Extend same character.
                # If previous run length r < L, we can append same char.
                for r in range(1, L):
                    v = dp0[r] + cost0
                    if v < ndp0[r + 1]:
                        ndp0[r + 1] = v
                    v = dp1[r] + cost1
                    if v < ndp1[r + 1]:
                        ndp1[r + 1] = v

                # Switch character: new run length 1.
                # From any previous 0-run, append 1.
                # From any previous 1-run, append 0.
                min_dp0 = min(dp0[1:])  # or min(dp0)
                min_dp1 = min(dp1[1:])
                v = min_dp1 + cost0
                if v < ndp0[1]:
                    ndp0[1] = v
                v = min_dp0 + cost1
                if v < ndp1[1]:
                    ndp1[1] = v

                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

            return min(min(dp0[1:]), min(dp1[1:])) <= numOps

        lo, hi = 1, n
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1
        return lo

Need verify min(dp0[1:]) when L=1: dp0[1:] length1. Good. min(dp0) includes index0 INF but no issue. But min(dp0[1:]) creates slice each iteration, overhead. Use min(dp0) because index0 INF. But if all INF? not. min(dp0) includes INF at index0, but if all other INF? not. If L=0 no. So use min(dp0). However, dp0[0] is INF; min(dp0) = min(INF, ...). If all other INF? impossible, but if it were, min would INF. Fine. Use min(dp0) to avoid slice allocation. But note dp0[0] = INF, and INF=1e9, but actual costs <=n <=1000, so min won't be INF if reachable. Good.

Let's maybe implement can(L) using a 2D list for clarity:
def can(L):
    dp = [[INF]*(L+1) for _ in range(2)]
    dp[0][1] = (arr[0] != 0)
    dp[1][1] = (arr[0] != 1)
    for bit in arr[1:]:
        ndp = [[INF]*(L+1) for _ in range(2)]
        for c in range(2):
            for r in range(1, L+1):
                cur = dp[c][r]
                if cur >= INF:
                    continue
                # same
                if r < L:
                    cost = 0 if bit == c else 1
                    nr = r+1
                    val = cur + cost
                    if val < ndp[c][nr]:
                        ndp[c][nr] = val
                # diff
                nc = 1 - c
                cost = 0 if bit == nc else 1
                val = cur + cost
                if val < ndp[nc][1]:
                    ndp[nc][1] = val
        dp = ndp
    return min(dp[0][1:]) <= numOps? min(dp[0]) <= numOps
This is simpler and less error-prone. But maybe slower due to nested loops and list-of-lists. n=1000, okay. However, final answer should be robust. Let's estimate worst-case with 2D list:
Binary search ~10 checks. For each check, positions ~1000, states 2*L. If L around500, state iterations ~1e6 per check, 10e6. Each state does two transitions and list indexing. 10e6 is fine. Actually if L=1000 for can(1000) not called maybe but can(999) could be called in some cases? Binary search mid sequence for n=1000 includes 999? Let's see if low=999 high=1000? mid=999, yes can(999) maybe called once. L=999, states ~1998, positions1000 =>2e6 states. Still fine. Total maybe <20 million. Python 20 million with inner loops maybe ~1s-2s. Acceptable.

But the two-array version with min(dp0) and same loop is also clear and faster. Let's decide final code. The two-array version is slightly more complex but still understandable. Need avoid bugs. Let's verify two-array version thoroughly.

Detailed two-array DP:

dp0[r] = min flips for processed prefix, valid, ending with '0' and last run length r.
dp1[r] = same ending with '1'.

Initialization:
dp0[1] = 0 if arr[0] == 0 else 1
dp1[1] = 0 if arr[0] == 1 else 1
Other INF.

For each next original bit `bit`:
We build ndp0, ndp1.

If we append '0':
- If previous char was '0' and previous run length r < L, new run length r+1. Cost = (bit != 0). This is same transition for dp0.
- If previous char was '1' (any run length), new run length 1. Cost = (bit != 0). This is diff transition from dp1.
If we append '1':
- If previous char was '1' and r < L, new run length r+1. Cost = (bit != 1). Same transition for dp1.
- If previous char was '0' (any run length), new run length 1. Cost = (bit != 1). Diff transition from dp0.

So:
cost0 = 0 if bit==0 else 1
cost1 = 0 if bit==1 else 1

Same:
for r in 1..L-1:
    ndp0[r+1] = min(ndp0[r+1], dp0[r] + cost0)
    ndp1[r+1] = min(ndp1[r+1], dp1[r] + cost1)

Diff:
min_dp0 = min(dp0)  # over r 0..L, index0 INF
min_dp1 = min(dp1)
ndp0[1] = min(ndp0[1], min_dp1 + cost0)
ndp1[1] = min(ndp1[1], min_dp0 + cost1)

Is there any diff transition from dp0 to ndp1[1] using min_dp0 + cost1. Yes. From dp1 to ndp0[1] using min_dp1 + cost0. Good.

What about appending same char from dp0[L]? Invalid, so loop excludes r=L. What about appending same char from dp0[0]? INF, ignore. Good.

At the end, min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps. Since index0 INF, okay.

Let's test two-array version with L=2, s="0000" we did. Good.

Let's test with L=1, s="0101":
first bit0: dp0[1]=0, dp1[1]=1.
bit1=1: cost0=1,cost1=0. same loop empty. min_dp0=0,min_dp1=1. ndp1[1]=0+0=0; ndp0[1]=1+1=2. dp0=2,dp1=0.
bit2=0: cost0=0,cost1=1. min_dp0=2,min_dp1=0. ndp0[1]=0+0=0; ndp1[1]=2+1=3. dp0=0,dp1=3.
bit3=1: cost0=1,cost1=0. min_dp0=0,min_dp1=3. ndp1[1]=0+0=0; ndp0[1]=3+1=4. min0. Good.

def can(L):
    INF = 10**9
    dp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
    dp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)
    dp0[1] = arr[0]
    dp1[1] = 1 - arr[0]
    for bit in arr[1:]:
        ndp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
        ndp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)
        cost0 = bit
        cost1 = 1 - bit
        # same
        # local variables for speed?
        for r in range(1, L):
            v = dp0[r] + cost0
            if v < ndp0[r + 1]:
                ndp0[r + 1] = v
            v = dp1[r] + cost1
            if v < ndp1[r + 1]:
                ndp1[r + 1] = v
        min_dp0 = min(dp0)
        min_dp1 = min(dp1)
        v = min_dp1 + cost0
        if v < ndp0[1]:
            ndp0[1] = v
        v = min_dp0 + cost1
        if v < ndp1[1]:
            ndp1[1] = v
        dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1
    return min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps

Let's decide final code:

class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        arr = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in s]

        # original maximum run
        max_run = 1
        cur = 1
        for i in range(1, n):
            if arr[i] == arr[i-1]:
                cur += 1
                if cur > max_run:
                    max_run = cur
            else:
                cur = 1

        def can(L: int) -> bool:
            INF = 10**9
            dp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
            dp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)
            dp0[1] = arr[0]
            dp1[1] = 1 - arr[0]

            for bit in arr[1:]:
                ndp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
                ndp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)
                cost0 = bit
                cost1 = 1 - bit

                # Append the same character as the previous one.
                for r in range(1, L):
                    v = dp0[r] + cost0
                    if v < ndp0[r + 1]:
                        ndp0[r + 1] = v
                    v = dp1[r] + cost1
                    if v < ndp1[r + 1]:
                        ndp1[r + 1] = v

                # Append the opposite character, starting a new run of length 1.
                min_dp0 = min(dp0)
                min_dp1 = min(dp1)

                v = min_dp1 + cost0
                if v < ndp0[1]:
                    ndp0[1] = v
                v = min_dp0 + cost1
                if v < ndp1[1]:
                    ndp1[1] = v

                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

            return min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps

        lo, hi = 1, max_run
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1
        return lo

Let's test with max_run=1, hi=1, return1. Good.

But is it possible that after operations, the longest identical substring length is larger than original max_run? We choose operations to minimize; doing nothing is allowed, so the minimum cannot be larger than original max_run. So safe.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "min_dp0 = min(dp0)" after same loop but
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def minLength(self, s: str, numOps: int) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        arr = [1 if ch == '1' else 0 for ch in s]

        # Original maximum run length is an upper bound, since doing nothing is allowed.
        max_run = 1
        cur = 1
        for i in range(1, n):
            if arr[i] == arr[i - 1]:
                cur += 1
                if cur > max_run:
                    max_run = cur
            else:
                cur = 1

        def can(L: int) -> bool:
            INF = 10 ** 9

            # dp0[r] / dp1[r]: min flips for processed prefix,
            # ending with '0' / '1' and current run length exactly r.
            dp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
            dp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)

            dp0[1] = arr[0]          # cost to make first char '0'
            dp1[1] = 1 - arr[0]      # cost to make first char '1'

            for bit in arr[1:]:
                ndp0 = [INF] * (L + 1)
                ndp1 = [INF] * (L + 1)

                cost0 = bit          # cost to append '0'
                cost1 = 1 - bit      # cost to append '1'

                # Append the same character, extending the current run.
                for r in range(1, L):
                    v = dp0[r] + cost0
                    if v < ndp0[r + 1]:
                        ndp0[r + 1] = v

                    v = dp1[r] + cost1
                    if v < ndp1[r + 1]:
                        ndp1[r + 1] = v

                # Append the opposite character, starting a new run of length 1.
                min_dp0 = min(dp0)
                min_dp1 = min(dp1)

                v = min_dp1 + cost0
                if v < ndp0[1]:
                    ndp0[1] = v

                v = min_dp0 + cost1
                if v < ndp1[1]:
                    ndp1[1] = v

                dp0, dp1 = ndp0, ndp1

            return min(min(dp0), min(dp1)) <= numOps

        lo, hi = 1, max_run
        while lo < hi:
            mid = (lo + hi) // 2
            if can(mid):
                hi = mid
            else:
                lo = mid + 1

        return lo
```
